. Then

\left|\frac{d}{ds}[(E_{\mathbf{I}}\varrho)\circ\gamma](s)\right|\leq C_{l}\langle\ln\theta\circ\gamma(s)\rangle^{c_{l}}\leq C_{l}\langle s\rangle^{c_{l}},

where we appealed to (
79
), (
81
) and the fact that
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
. This estimate can be integrated in
order to deduce that (
82
) holds. In order to obtain these estimates, we, again, appealed to (
79
) and
the fact that
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
.

In order to estimate the derivatives of
\ln\theta
, note that (
78
) yields

\exp(2\varrho+2\ln\theta)=b_{11}\cdots b_{nn}+R,

where
R
, up to signs, consists of terms containing
k\geq 1
factors of the form
\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B})}b_{AB}
,
A>B
, and
n-k
factors of the
form
b_{AB}
,
A\leq B
. Differentiating this equality once yields

(86)

2\exp(2\varrho+2\ln\theta)E_{i}\varrho+2\exp(2\varrho+2\ln\theta)E_{i}\ln\theta=E_{i}(b_{11}\cdots b_{nn})+E_{i}R.

Note that the factors
\exp(2\varrho+2\ln\theta)
converge to
1
due to (
79
). Moreover, the first term on the right hand
side converges to zero as
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
. Consider
E_{i}R
. It contains terms with two types of factors. Either
E_{i}
hits

\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B})}b_{AB}
,
A>B
, in which case the result is

-2E_{i}(p_{A}-p_{B})\ln\theta\cdot\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B})}b_{AB}-2(p_{A}-p_{B})E_{i}(\ln\theta)\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B})}b_{AB}+\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B})}E_{i}b_{AB}.

The first term converges to zero faster than
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
, the second term can be written as
E_{i}(\ln\theta)
times a factor that
converges to zero faster than
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
, and the last term converges to zero faster than
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
. Second, if
E_{i}
hits
a factor of the form
b_{AB}
,
A\leq B
, then the result converges to zero at least at the rate
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
. To conclude, the right hand
side of the equality (
86
) contains a term including the factor
E_{i}\ln\theta
. However, in this term, the factor multiplying

E_{i}\ln\theta
converges to zero faster than
\theta^{-\varepsilon}
. The term on the right hand side including the factor
E_{i}\ln\theta
can thus be
combined with the second term on the left hand side. This yields a bound of
E_{i}\ln\theta
in terms of
E_{i}\varrho
and decaying terms.
In order to estimate higher order derivatives, one can proceed similarly, differentiating (
86
) further. By an inductive
argument, all but the highest order derivatives appearing on the right hand side can be estimated. The highest order derivative of
\ln\theta

appearing on the right hand side will, in analogy with the above, be multiplied by a factor converging to zero. This can then be moved to the
left hand side, as in the first step. At the end, we then obtain the estimate (
83
).
∎

5.2.
Components of the eigenvector fields

Next, introduce the matrices
X_{A}^{B}
and
Y_{A}^{B}
by the requirement that
X_{A}=X_{A}^{B}\mathscr{X}_{B}
and
\mathscr{X}_{A}=Y_{A}^{B}X_{B}
, where

\{\mathscr{X}_{A}\}
and
\{X_{A}\}
are defined in the statement of Theorem
28
. Then

(87)

X_{A}^{B}=\mathscr{Y}^{B}(X_{A}),\ \ \ Y_{A}^{B}=Y^{B}(\mathscr{X}_{A}),\ \ \ X_{A}^{B}Y_{B}^{C}=Y_{A}^{B}X_{B}^{C}=\delta^{C}_{A}.

These matrices satisfy the following relations.

Lemma 65
.

Given the assumptions and notation of Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{k}
,
such that

(88)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{k}(X^{A}_{B}-\delta^{A}_{B})|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}\min\{1,\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\},

(89)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{k}(Y^{A}_{B}-\delta^{A}_{B})|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}\min\{1,\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\},

(90)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{k}(\bar{\mu}_{A}+p_{A}\ln\theta)|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
for all
A
and
B
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that (
20a
), (
20b
) and the definition of
X_{A}
,
Y^{A}
,
\mathscr{X}_{A}
and

\mathscr{Y}^{A}
yield the conclusion that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there are constants
C_{k}
and
\eta>0
such that

(91)

|\bar{D}^{k}[\mathscr{Y}^{A}(X_{B})-\delta^{A}_{B}]|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}[Y^{A}(\mathscr{X}_{B})-\delta^{A}_{B}]|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}(b_{AB}-\delta_{AB})|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
for all
A
,
B
. In particular, (
88
) and (
89
) hold in case
B\leq A
. On the other hand,

(92)

\textstyle{\sum}_{A,B}b_{AB}\theta^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}}\mathscr{Y}^{A}\otimes\mathscr{Y}^{B}=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}e^{2\bar{\mu}_{C}}Y^{C}\otimes Y^{C}.

Evaluating this equality on
(\mathscr{X}_{A},X_{A})
yields

\textstyle{\sum}_{B}b_{AB}\theta^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}}\mathscr{Y}^{B}(X_{A})=e^{2\bar{\mu}_{A}}Y^{A}(\mathscr{X}_{A})

(no summation on
A
). Combining this equality with (
83
) and (
91
) yields
(
90
). Next, evaluating (
92
) on
(\mathscr{X}_{A},X_{C})
with
C<A
yields

\textstyle{\sum}_{B}b_{AB}\theta^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}}\mathscr{Y}^{B}(X_{C})=e^{2\bar{\mu}_{C}}Y^{C}(\mathscr{X}_{A}).

Combining this equality with (
90
) and (
91
) yields (
89
).

In order to prove (
88
), note that
YX=\mathrm{Id}
, where
X
and
Y
are the matrices with components

X^{A}_{B}
and
Y^{A}_{B}
respectively. Next, note that
Y=U+L
, where
U
is upper triangular,
L
is lower triangular and
the diagonal components of
L
vanish. Moreover, for
A>B
,
L_{A}^{B}=O(\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B}+\eta)})
in any
C^{m}
-norm. Moreover,

U-\mathrm{Id}=O(\theta^{-2\eta})
in any
C^{m}
-norm. In particular,

X=Y^{-1}=(\mathrm{Id}+U^{-1}L)^{-1}U^{-1};

note that
\|U^{-1}L\|\rightarrow 0
in the direction of the singularity, so that we can assume
\|U^{-1}L\|\leq 1/2
in the arguments
to follow. Let
R:=-U^{-1}L
. Then

X=\textstyle{\sum}_{k=0}^{\infty}R^{k}U^{-1}.

Note that
R^{k}=O(\theta^{-2k\eta})
in any
C^{m}
norm. Moreover, for
k
large enough, say
k\geq K
,
2k\eta\geq 2(p_{n}-p_{1}+\eta)

on
\bar{M}
. It is therefore straightforward to prove the desired estimate for the terms with
k\geq K
, and we only need to focus on a
finite number of terms of the form
R^{k}U^{-1}
. However, it can be verified that the components of
R
satisfy
R_{A}^{n}=0
,
A=1,\dots,n
,
and, for
B<n
,

R_{A}^{B}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}O(\theta^{-2(p_{B+1}-p_{B}+\eta)})&B\geq A,\\
O(\theta^{-2(p_{A}-p_{B}+\eta)})&B<A.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, this holds in any
C^{m}
norm. It can be verified that this structure is preserved when taking positive integer powers. Finally,
when multiplying a matrix with this structure on the right with
U^{-1}
(which is upper triangular), the lower triangular part of the resulting
matrix satisfies the same conditions as the lower triangular part of
R
(i.e., the estimates satisfied by
R_{A}^{B}
,
B<A
, are also satisfied
by
(R^{m}U^{-1})_{A}^{B}
for
m\geq 1
and
B<A
). It follows that (
88
) holds for
B>A
. The lemma follows.
∎

Using this information, we are in a position to prove that (
28b
) holds.

Lemma 66
.

Given the assumptions and notation of Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
a constant
C_{k}
, such that

(93)

|D^{k}[\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})-p_{B}\delta^{A}_{B}]|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}e^{-2\eta}\min\{1,\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\}

(no summation on
B
) on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that

\mathcal{K}^{A}_{B}:=\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}\ell_{C}\mathscr{Y}^{A}(X_{C})Y^{C}(\mathscr{X}_{B})=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}\ell_{C}X^{A}_{C}Y^{C}_{B},

where
\ell_{A}
is the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{K}
corresponding to
X_{A}
.
Note also that
\ell_{A}-p_{A}=O(\theta^{-2\eta})
in any
C^{k}
-norm due to (
20a
). Combining these observations with
Lemma
65
yields
\mathcal{K}^{A}_{A}=p_{A}+O(\theta^{-2\eta})
(no summation on
A
) in any
C^{k}
-norm. Similarly,

\mathcal{K}_{B}^{A}=O(\theta^{-2\eta})
for
B<A
in any
C^{k}
-norm. Finally, for
A<B
, the desired conclusion again follows by appealing to
Lemma
65
. The desired estimate follows.
∎

Next, define
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
by
[X_{B},X_{C}]=\lambda_{BC}^{A}X_{A}
. We wish to estimate
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
in case
1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C}\leq 0
; cf. the
definition of quiescent initial data on the singularity.

Lemma 67
.

Given the assumptions and notation of Theorem
28
, fix
\bar{x}\in\bar{M}
and assume that

1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})\leq 0
for some
A
and
B\neq C
. Then there is a neighbourhood
U
of
\bar{x}
in
\bar{M}
, an
\eta>0
and,
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{k}
such that

(94)

|D^{k}(\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}\lambda^{A}_{BC})|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
U\times(0,t_{0}]
.

Remark 68
.

Assuming, in addition to the conditions of the lemma, that
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
constant
C_{k}
, such that

(95)

|D^{k}(\lambda^{1}_{23})|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2(p_{2}-p_{1}+\eta)}

on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Compute

(96)

Y^{A}([X_{B},X_{C}])=X_{B}^{D}\mathscr{X}_{D}(X^{E}_{C})Y^{A}_{E}-X_{C}^{E}\mathscr{X}_{E}(X^{D}_{B})Y^{A}_{D}+X^{D}_{B}X^{E}_{C}Y^{A}_{F}\gamma^{F}_{DE},

where
\gamma^{F}_{DE}=\mathscr{Y}^{F}([\mathscr{X}_{D},\mathscr{X}_{E}])
. Assume that
1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})\leq 0
. In order for this to hold, we have to
have
B>A
and
C>A
; cf. Remark
31
. In what follows, we also assume, without loss of generality, that
B<C
. Combining these
observations with
Lemma
65
, it is clear that the first two terms on the right hand side of (
96
) are

O(\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A}+\eta)})
in any
C^{k}
norm. In particular, multiplying the first two terms on the right hand side of (
96
)
with
\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}
, it is clear that the resulting expression is
O(\theta^{-2(1-p_{C}+\eta)})
in any
C^{k}
-norm. Next, consider

\begin{split}&\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}X^{D}_{B}X^{E}_{C}Y^{A}_{F}\gamma^{F}_{DE}\\
=&(\theta^{2(p_{B}-p_{D})}X^{D}_{B})\cdot(\theta^{2(p_{C}-p_{E})}X^{E}_{C})\cdot(\theta^{2(p_{F}-p_{A})}Y^{A}_{F})\cdot(\theta^{-2(1+p_{F}-p_{D}-p_{E})}\gamma^{F}_{DE}).\end{split}

Due to Lemma
65
, the first three factors on the right hand side are bounded in any
C^{k}
norm. In order for the last term to
be non-zero,
1+p_{F}-p_{D}-p_{E}
has to be strictly positive in a neighbourhood of
\bar{x}
. The estimate (
94
) follows. The
proof of (
95
) is similar.
∎

Corollary 69
.

Given the assumptions and notation of Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant

C_{k}
such that

(97)

|D^{k}(\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}\lambda^{A}_{BC})|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
for all
A
,
B
and
C
.

Remark 70
.

Since
\lambda_{BC}^{A}
is well defined up to a sign, the estimate (
97
) is globally meaningful.

Proof.

If
B=C
, there is nothing to prove. In what follows, we therefore assume
B\neq C
. Fix an
\bar{x}\in\bar{M}
. Given
A
,
B
,
C
such that
B\neq C
,
there are then two possibilities. Either
1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})\leq 0

or
1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})>0
. In the first case, we can appeal to Lemma
67
in order to conclude
that there is an open neighbourhood
U_{ABC}
and an
\eta_{ABC}>0
such that (
94
), with
\eta
replaced by
\eta_{ABC}
,
holds in
U_{ABC}\times(0,t_{0}]
. In case
1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})>0
, there is trivially such an open neighbourhood

U_{ABC}
and
\eta_{ABC}>0
. Taking the intersection of all these
U_{ABC}
yields an open neighbourhood
U_{\bar{x}}
and taking the minimum
of all the
\eta_{ABC}
yields an
\eta_{\bar{x}}>0
. Since
\bar{M}
is compact, there is a finite cover by sets of the form
U_{\bar{x}}
. Taking the
minimum of the associated
\eta_{\bar{x}}
yields an
\eta>0
such that the statement of the corollary holds.
∎

Using this information, we estimate the conformally rescaled spatial Ricci curvature.

Proposition 71
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. Let, using the notation of
Theorem
28
,
\bar{R}
denote the spatial Ricci
tensor, viewed as a family of
(1,1)
-tensor fields,
\bar{S}
denote the spatial scalar curvature,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}:=\theta^{-2}\bar{R}
and

\bar{\mathcal{S}}:=\theta^{-2}\bar{S}
. Then there is an
\eta>0
and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
C_{k}
such that

(98)

|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 72
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the proposition,
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, then there are, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constants

\alpha_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(99)

|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})}

on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

To estimate
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
, it is convenient to appeal to
[
69
, Corollary 125, p. 73]
. The
\mu_{A}
appearing in this corollary
are given by
\mu_{A}=\bar{\mu}_{A}+\ln\theta
, so that
\bar{D}^{k}[\mu_{A}-(1-p_{A})\ln\theta]=O(\theta^{-2\eta})

due to (
90
). Note that
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
(the
\gamma^{A}_{BC}
appearing in
[
69
, Corollary 125, p. 73]
should be
replaced by
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
here) and
a_{A}
are bounded in any
C^{k}
-norm and that
\check{a}_{A}
,
\bar{\mu}_{A}

and
\mu_{A}
do not grow faster than polynomially in
\ln\theta
in any
C^{k}
-norm. In particular, any number of
X_{A}
derivatives of these
objects grows at worst polynomially. Note also that
p_{A}<1
due to Remark
31
. Combining these observations
with
[
69
, (301)–(306), p. 73]
, it is clear that for

k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
\alpha_{k}
such that most of the terms on the right hand side of
[
69
, (301), p. 73]

decay to zero as
O(\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{n})})
in
C^{k}
. However, there are some terms containing factors of
the form
e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}
. On the other hand, terms that contain a factor
e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}
also contain a factor

\lambda^{A}_{BC}
(or a spatial derivative thereof). In order to estimate the corresponding terms, we can thus appeal to
Corollary
69
. In order to illustrate the arguments required, consider, e.g.,

\begin{split}&e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}X_{D}(\lambda^{A}_{BC})\\
=&e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}\theta^{2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}X_{D}(\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}\lambda^{A}_{BC})\\
&+e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}\theta^{2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}X_{D}[2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})\ln\theta](\theta^{-2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})}\lambda^{A}_{BC}).\end{split}

Due to Corollary
69
, terms of this form decay as desired in any
C^{k}
-norm; since

\mu_{A}-(1-p_{A})\ln\theta=O(\theta^{-2\eta})
, the product of the first two factors is, in each term, bounded in any
C^{k}
-norm;
the third factor in the second term does not grow faster than polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm; and the last factor decays as

\theta^{-2\eta}
in any
C^{k}
-norm in each of the terms.

In case
n=3
, a more detailed argument can be carried out. In fact, then, if
1\in\{B,C\}
,
e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}
decays to zero as

O(\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})})
in
C^{k}
. Assume, therefore,
that
\{B,C\}=\{2,3\}
. If
A\in\{2,3\}
, we again obtain the same decay. The only interesting terms are thus the ones containing one
factor of the form
e^{2\mu_{1}-2\mu_{2}-2\mu_{3}}
and a factor of the form
\lambda^{1}_{23}
or a spatial derivative thereof. However, we know
that
\lambda^{1}_{23}=O(\theta^{-2(p_{2}-p_{1}+\eta)})
(and similarly for every
C^{k}
-norm); cf. Remark
68
.
Thus the potentially problematic terms are
O(\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})})
(and similarly for every
C^{k}
-norm).

Writing

\bar{\mathcal{R}}=\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{A}_{B}X_{A}\otimes Y^{B},

the desired estimate for
\bar{\mathcal{R}}
follows. Due to
[
69
, Lemma 122, p. 69]
, the proof of the estimate for
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is essentially
identical.
∎

Next, we wish to estimate the contribution from the lapse function to
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
. Introduce, to this end, the
(1,1)
-tensor field

\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, given by

(100)

\bar{\mathcal{N}}^{A}_{B}:=\theta^{-2}N^{-1}\overline{\nabla}^{A}\overline{\nabla}_{B}N.

Here
\overline{\nabla}
is the Levi-Civita connection induced on the hypersurfaces of constant
t
.

Lemma 73
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied and let
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
be defined by
(
100
). Then, using the notation of Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and,
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
C_{k}
such that

(101)

|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{N}}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 74
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the proposition,
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, then there are, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constants

\alpha_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

|\bar{D}^{k}\bar{\mathcal{N}}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})}

on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Note, first of all, that the derivatives of
\ln N
can be bounded by appealing to (
26
) and
(
83
). Combining this observation with
[
69
, Lemma 127, p. 74]
and the assumptions, the proof
of the statement is similar to, but simpler than, the proof of Proposition
71
.
∎

Next, we estimate
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
.

Proposition 75
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. Then, using the notation of
Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a
C_{k}
such that

(102)

|\bar{D}^{k}[q-(n-1)]|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 76
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the proposition,
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, then there are, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constants

\alpha_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(103)

|\bar{D}^{k}(q-2)|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 77
.

When appealing to the results of
[
68
]
, we need upper bounds on the eigenvalues of
\check{K}
, the Weingarten map of the conformally
rescaled metric
\hat{g}:=\theta^{2}g
. Combining
[
68
, (3.3), p. 26]
with
[
68
, (3.4), p. 26]
, we conclude that

\check{K}=\mathcal{K}-(q+1)\mathrm{Id}/n
. Since
q
converges to
n-1
, cf. (
102
), we conclude that the eigenvalues of
\check{K}

converge to
p_{A}-1
. In particular, they are all negative, cf. Remark
31
, so that
\check{K}
has a silent upper bound
asymptotically; cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.10, p. 27]
.

Proof.

The basis for the estimate of
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
is the formula for
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
given by
[
69
, (270), p. 65]
:

(104)

\begin{split}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}=&-\left(\frac{\Delta_{\bar{g}}N}{\theta^{2}N}+\frac{n}{n-1}\frac{\rho-\bar{p}+2\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}-\frac{\bar{S}}{\theta^{2}}\right)\mathcal{K}^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}\\
&+\frac{1}{(n-1)\theta^{2}}\left(\rho-\bar{p}+2\Lambda\right)\delta^{i}_{j}+\frac{1}{N\theta^{2}}\overline{\nabla}^{i}\overline{\nabla}_{j}N+\theta^{-2}\mathcal{P}^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}-\frac{\bar{R}^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}}{\theta^{2}}.\end{split}

Several of the terms can be estimated by appealing to (
98
), (
101
) and the convergence of
\mathcal{K}
. However, there
are three types of exceptions: terms including a factor of
the form
\theta^{-2}(\rho-\bar{p})
, a factor of the form
\Lambda/\theta^{2}
or a factor of the form
\theta^{-2}\mathcal{P}
. Estimates for terms including
a factor of the form
\Lambda/\theta^{2}
follow immediately from (
83
). To estimate the remaining terms, it is sufficient
to appeal to
[
69
, Remark 81, p. 49]
; given the corresponding formulae and (
26
), it is clear that we can proceed
as in the previous proofs in order to derive the desired estimate for
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
.

The estimate for
q-(n-1)
can be derived by a similar argument due to
[
69
, (266), p. 65]
.
∎

In order to refine this conclusion, it is of interest to consider the components of
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
with respect to the frame
\{\mathscr{X}_{A}\}
.

Lemma 78
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. Then, using the notation of
Theorem
28
, there is an
\eta>0
and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{k}
such that

(105)

|\bar{D}^{k}[(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})]|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}\min\{1,\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 79
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the lemma,
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, then there are, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constants

\alpha_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

|\bar{D}^{k}[(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})]|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})}\min\{1,\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\}

on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

For
A\geq B
, the estimates are immediate consequences of (
102
) and (
103
). We therefore need
to focus on the case
B>A
. Define, for
A\neq B
,

(106)

\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}:=\frac{1}{\ell_{B}-\ell_{A}}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(Y^{A},X_{B});

cf.
[
68
, (6.8), p. 68]
. Due to Proposition
75
, we know that the
C^{k}
-norm of
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}
is bounded by the
right hand side of (
102
). On the other hand,
[
68
, (6.19), p. 70]
implies that
e^{\bar{\mu}_{A}-\bar{\mu}_{B}}\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}
is
antisymmetric for
A\neq B
. In particular, if
A<B
, then

(107)

\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}=-e^{2(\bar{\mu}_{B}-\bar{\mu}_{A})}\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}.

There is thus an
\eta>0
and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{k}
such that

(108)

|\bar{D}^{k}\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}[(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(Y^{A},X_{B})]|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
if
A<B
, where we appealed to (
90
) and to (
106
) in order to estimate the second term on the
left hand side of (
108
). In case
n=3
and
\mathscr{O}^{1}_{23}=0
on
\bar{M}
, this estimate can be improved to

(109)

|\bar{D}^{k}\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|_{\bar{h}}+|\bar{D}^{k}[(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(Y^{A},X_{B})]|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln\theta\rangle^{\alpha_{k}}\theta^{-2(p_{B}-p_{A})}\theta^{-2(1-p_{3})}

on
M_{0}
if
A<B
, where
\alpha_{k}
and
C_{k}
are constants.

Next, note that

(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})=X_{C}^{A}Y^{D}_{B}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(Y^{C},X_{D}).

Combining this observation with Lemma
65
, (
108
) and (
109
) yields
the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

5.3.
Scalar field

Next, we wish to analyse the asymptotic behaviour of the scalar field. We do so by appealing to the results of
[
68
]
.

Lemma 80
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. Then, using the notation of
Theorem
28
, there are
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M})
,
an
\eta>0
and, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{k}
such that

(110)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{k}(\hat{U}\phi-\Phi_{1})|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta},

(111)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{k}(\phi+\Phi_{1}\ln\theta-\Phi_{0})|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
.

Remark 81
.

Due to (
84
), (
111
) can be written

(112)

|\bar{D}^{k}(\phi-\Phi_{1}\varrho-\Phi_{0})|_{\bar{h}}\leq C_{k}\theta^{-2\eta}

on
M_{0}
.

Proof.

The statement essentially follows by appealing to the results of
[
68
]
. For this reason, the proof mainly consists of a verification
that the corresponding conditions are satisfied. Let us begin by verifying that the basic assumptions, cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.27, p. 34]

are satisfied.

The basic assumptions.
It is clear that by restricting
(M,g)
close enough to the big bang, it is time oriented, it has an expanding
partial pointed foliation and
\mathcal{K}
is non-degenerate. In what follows, it is, however, important to keep in mind that in
[
68
]
,

\varrho
,
\bar{D}
etc. are defined with respect to the reference metric
\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}
introduced in
[
68
]
(and
\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}
is typically
different from
\bar{h}
). We therefore here
write
\varrho_{\mathrm{ref}}
to indicate the logarithmic volume density defined with respect to
\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}
. On the other hand, the difference

\varrho-\varrho_{\mathrm{ref}}
is independent of
t
. Due to
[
68
, Lemma A.1, p. 201]
, we can assume
\mathcal{K}
to have a global frame;
this is achieved by considering a finite covering space, if necessary, an operation which does not affect the local conclusions concerning the
asymptotics. That
\check{K}
has a silent upper bound follows from Remark
77
. Due to (
84
) and
Proposition
75
,
it is clear that
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
satisfies a weak off diagonal exponential bound; cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.19, p. 29]
(in this definition,
the requirement that the constants
C_{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{od}}
,
G_{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{od}}
and
M_{\mathcal{K},\mathrm{od}}
be strictly positive is not necessary; it is sufficient if they
are
\geq 0
). Since
\mathcal{K}
converges exponentially in any
C^{k}
-norm and
since
\chi=0
in our setting, it is clear that
[
68
, (3.28), p. 34]
and
[
68
, (3.29), p. 34]
hold. Moreover, by assumption,

[
68
, (3.18), p. 32]
holds. Thus the basic assumptions are satisfied; cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.27, p. 34]
.

Higher order Sobolev assumptions.
Next, let us verify that the higher order Sobolev assumptions are satisfied; cf.

[
68
, Definition 3.28, p. 34]
. Due to the assumptions concerning the derivatives of
\ln\hat{N}
and the fact that
\langle\varrho_{\mathrm{ref}}\rangle

and
\langle\ln\theta\rangle
are equivalent, cf. (
84
), it is clear that
\ln\hat{N}
satisfies the required bound (in what follows,
the
\mathfrak{u}
appearing in the definition of the higher order Sobolev assumptions will be allowed to depend on the number of derivatives,
say
l
). Since
\hat{U}\ln N
does not grow faster than polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm by assumption,
and the same is true of
\hat{U}(\ln\theta)=-(1+q)/n
(due to (
102
)), it is clear that
\hat{U}\ln\hat{N}
does not grow faster than
polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm. Combining these observations with the fact that
\chi=0
and the fact that (
20a
),
(
83
) and (
102
) hold, it follows that all the conditions of

[
68
, Definition 3.28, p. 34]
are satisfied. In other words, for any choice of
l
, there is a
0\leq\mathfrak{u}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
the
(\mathfrak{u}_{l},l)
-Sobolev assumptions are satisfied.

Higher order
C^{k}
-assumptions.
Due to the arguments of the previous paragraph, there is, for any choice of
l
, a

0\leq\mathfrak{u}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the
(\mathfrak{u}_{l},l)
-supremum assumptions are satisfied; cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.31, p. 35]
.

The standard assumptions.
Since
\chi=0
, it is clear that the conditions of
[
68
, Lemma 3.33, p. 36]
are satisfied.
In particular, the standard assumptions are thus satisfied; cf.
[
68
, Definition 3.36, p. 36]
.

Basic energy estimate.
Next, we wish to appeal to
[
68
, Proposition 14.24, p. 158]
. Note, to this end, that, due to the
above observations and the assumptions, the conditions of
[
68
, Lemma 7.5, p. 76]
,
[
68
, Lemma 7.12, p. 81]
and

[
68
, Lemma 7.13, p. 83]

are all satisfied. Since we are here interested in the wave equation, the estimates
[
68
, (3.32), p. 35]
and
[
68
, (3.34), p. 35]

are trivially satisfied. Next, note that if
\kappa_{1}
is the smallest integer strictly larger than
n/2+1
, then, for a suitable choice of

\mathfrak{u}
, the
(\mathfrak{u},\kappa_{1})
-supremum assumptions are satisfied. Given
1\leq l\in\mathbb{Z}
and a suitable choice of
\mathfrak{u}_{l}
, the

(\mathfrak{u}_{l},l)
-Sobolev assumptions are also satisfied. Since
\phi
satisfies the wave equation, it satisfies
[
68
, (12.32), p. 124]

with vanishing right hand side,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{0}=0
,
\hat{\mathcal{X}}^{A}=0
and
\hat{\alpha}=0
.
Due to Proposition
75
, it is also clear that
[
68
, (7.78), p. 84]
holds. Since

\alpha
(using the notation of
[
68
, (1.1), p. 7]
) vanishes, it is clear that
[
68
, (11.7), p. 110]
holds. That

[
68
, (11.39), p. 115]
holds is trivial due to the fact that the left hand side of this estimate vanishes identically. Summing up,
the conditions of
[
68
, Proposition 14.24, p. 158]
are satisfied. In particular, for any
1\leq l\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are constants

a_{l}
and
C_{l}
such that

(113)

\hat{G}_{l}(\tau)\leq C_{l}\langle\tau\rangle^{a_{l}}

for
\tau\leq 0
. Here
\tau(t):=\varrho_{\mathrm{ref}}(\bar{x}_{0},t)
for some reference point
\bar{x}_{0}\in\bar{M}
. Moreover,

(114)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\left(|\hat{U}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\phi)|^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{-2\mu_{A}}|X_{A}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\phi)|^{2}+\langle\tau\rangle^{-3}|E_{\mathbf{I}}\phi|^{2}\right)

(115)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{k}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{M}_{\tau}}\mathcal{E}_{k}\mu_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

Here
\{E_{i}\}
is a global frame on the tangent space of
\bar{M}
and if
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
is such that
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, then

E_{\mathbf{I}}:=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}
and
|\mathbf{I}|=k
. One immediate consequence of (
113
) is that
\phi
does not grow faster than
polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm. However, this is already part of the assumptions. More interestingly, combining this estimate with

[
68
, Corollary 13.8, p. 130]
and
[
68
, Remark 13.9, p. 130]
yields the conclusion that
\hat{U}\phi
does not grow faster than
polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm. Combining this estimate with
[
69
, (165), p. 51]
and arguments similar to those of previous
lemmas, it follows that
\hat{U}^{2}\phi
decays as
\theta^{-2\eta}
in any
C^{k}
-norm.

Next, note that

\partial_{\tau}[\hat{U}(\phi)]=\xi\hat{U}^{2}(\phi),

where
\xi:=\hat{N}/\partial_{t}\tau
. Since
\xi
is bounded due to
[
68
, (7.86), p. 85]
, and since the spatial derivatives of
\ln\hat{N}
do not grow
faster than polynomially, it is clear that
\xi
does not grow faster than polynomially in any
C^{k}
-norm. Thus
\partial_{\tau}[\hat{U}(\phi)]

decays exponentially in any
C^{k}
-norm. In particular,
\hat{U}(\phi)
converges exponentially in any
C^{k}
-norm. This guarantees the existence
of a
\Phi_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M})
such that (
110
) holds. Next, consider

(116)

\hat{U}(\phi-\Phi_{1}\varrho)=\hat{U}(\phi)-\Phi_{1},

where we appealed to
[
68
, (7.9), p. 74]
. Since the right hand side converges to zero exponentially in every
C^{k}
-norm, we conclude
the existence of a
\Phi_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M})
such that (
112
), and thereby (
111
), holds.
∎

5.4.
The limits of the constraint equations in the case of a scalar field

Next, we calculate the limits of the constraint equations in the presence of a scalar field.

Lemma 82
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. Then, using the notation of
Theorem
28
, the limits of the renormalised Hamiltonian and momentum constraints can be written

(117)

\Phi_{1}^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}^{2}=1,\ \ \ \mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}}\mathscr{K}=\Phi_{1}d\Phi_{0}

respectively, where
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}
are the functions whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
80
.

Proof.

Due to
[
69
, (8), p. 7]
, the Hamiltonian constraint implies that

(118)

1=2\Omega+2\Omega_{\Lambda}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}-\theta^{-2}\bar{S},

where
\Omega=\rho/\theta^{2}
,
\rho
denotes the energy density associated with the scalar field,
\Omega_{\Lambda}=\Lambda/\theta^{2}
and
\bar{S}

denotes the spatial scalar curvature. It is clear that
\Omega_{\Lambda}
converges to zero. Moreover, due to

[
69
, (151), p. 49]
and the conclusions of Lemma
80
, it is clear that
2\Omega
converges
to
\Phi_{1}^{2}
. Due to (
98
), it is also clear that
\theta^{-2}\bar{S}
converges to zero. Finally,
\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}
equals
the sum of the squares of the
\ell_{A}
. This converges to the sum of the squares of the
p_{A}
. To conclude, the limit of the expansion
normalised Hamiltonian constraint is the first equality in (
117
).

Next, note that the momentum constraint can be written

\overline{\nabla}_{m}\mathcal{K}^{m}_{\phantom{m}j}+\mathcal{K}^{m}_{\phantom{m}j}\overline{\nabla}_{m}\ln\theta-\overline{\nabla}_{j}\ln\theta=\hat{U}(\phi)\overline{\nabla}_{j}\phi.

Compute

\overline{\nabla}_{A}\mathcal{K}^{B}_{\phantom{B}C}=X_{A}(\mathcal{K}^{B}_{\phantom{B}C})-\mathcal{K}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}Y^{B},X_{C})-\mathcal{K}(Y^{B},\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C}).

Choosing
B=A
and summing over
A
, the first term on the right hand side becomes
X_{C}(\ell_{C})
(no summation). Next,

\begin{split}-\mathcal{K}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}Y^{A},X_{C})=&-\ell_{C}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}Y^{A})(X_{C})=\ell_{C}Y^{A}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C})\\
=&\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{C}e^{-2\bar{\mu}_{A}}\langle\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C},X_{A}\rangle\end{split}

(no summation on
C
). However,

\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{-2\bar{\mu}_{A}}\langle\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C},X_{A}\rangle=\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{-2\bar{\mu}_{A}}\langle\lambda_{AC}^{D}X_{D}+\overline{\nabla}_{X_{C}}X_{A},X_{A}\rangle=\textstyle{\sum}_{A}X_{C}(\bar{\mu}_{A})+\lambda^{A}_{AC},

where
[X_{B},X_{C}]=\lambda_{BC}^{A}X_{A}
. To conclude

-\mathcal{K}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}Y^{A},X_{C})=\ell_{C}X_{C}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\bar{\mu}_{A}\right)+\ell_{C}\lambda^{A}_{AC}

(no summation on
C
). Next,

\begin{split}-\mathcal{K}(Y^{A},\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C})=&-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}Y^{A}(\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C})=-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}e^{-2\bar{\mu}_{A}}\langle\overline{\nabla}_{X_{A}}X_{C},X_{A}\rangle\\
=&-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}\lambda^{A}_{AC}-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}X_{C}(\bar{\mu}_{A}).\end{split}

The momentum constraint can thus be rewritten

\begin{split}&X_{C}(\ell_{C})+\ell_{C}\lambda^{A}_{AC}-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}\lambda^{A}_{AC}+(\ell_{C}-1)X_{C}(\ln\theta)\\
&+\ell_{C}X_{C}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\bar{\mu}_{A}\right)-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}X_{C}(\bar{\mu}_{A})=\hat{U}(\phi)X_{C}(\phi)\end{split}

(no summation on
C
). The first three terms on the left hand side have finite limits. However, the same is not in general true of the last
three terms on the left hand side, nor is it in general true of the right hand side. However, it is of interest to consider

(119)

\ell_{C}X_{C}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\bar{\mu}_{A}+\ln\theta\right).

Due to (
90
) and the fact that the sum of the
p_{A}
equals
1
, it is clear that the expression (
119
)
converges to zero exponentially. Next, consider

\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}X_{C}(\bar{\mu}_{A})+\hat{U}(\phi)X_{C}(\phi)+X_{C}(\ln\theta).

Since
\ell_{A}
converges to
p_{A}
exponentially, since (
90
) holds, since
\hat{U}(\phi)
converges to
\Phi_{1}
exponentially,
and since
\phi+\Phi_{1}\ln\theta-\Phi_{0}
converges to zero exponentially in any
C^{k}
-norm, this expression equals

\begin{split}&\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}X_{C}(-p_{A}\ln\theta)+\Phi_{1}X_{C}(-\Phi_{1}\ln\theta+\Phi_{0})+X_{C}(\ln\theta)\\
=&\left[-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}X_{C}(p_{A})-\Phi_{1}X_{C}(\Phi_{1})\right]\ln\theta-\left[\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}\right]X_{C}(\ln\theta)\\
&+\Phi_{1}X_{C}(\Phi_{0})+X_{C}(\ln\theta)=\Phi_{1}X_{C}(\Phi_{0})\end{split}

up to terms that decay exponentially in any
C^{k}
-norm. Here we used the fact that
\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}=1
, so that

\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}X_{C}(p_{A})+\Phi_{1}X_{C}(\Phi_{1})=0.

Summing up, the expansion normalised limit of the momentum constraint is

\mathscr{X}_{C}(p_{C})+p_{C}\gamma^{A}_{AC}-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}\gamma^{A}_{AC}=\Phi_{1}\mathscr{X}_{C}(\Phi_{0})

(no summation on
C
), where
\gamma^{A}_{BC}
are the structure constants associated with the frame
\{\mathscr{X}_{A}\}
. On the other hand, an argument
similar to the above yields

(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}}\mathscr{K})(\mathscr{X}_{C})=\mathscr{X}_{C}(p_{C})+p_{C}\gamma^{A}_{AC}-\textstyle{\sum}_{A}p_{A}\gamma^{A}_{AC}

(no summation on
C
); cf. the beginning of the proof of Lemma
59
.
The limit of the momentum constraint can thus be written
\mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}}\mathscr{K}=\Phi_{1}d\Phi_{0}
.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Theorem
28

Finally, we are in a position to prove Theorem
28
.

Theorem
28
.

The estimate (
27
) follows from (
84
) and (
90
). The estimate
(
28b
) follows from (
93
). The estimates (
28a
) and (
29
)
follow from (
102
). The estimate (
28c
) follows from (
105
). The existence of

\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}
as well as the conclusion that the asymptotic versions of the constraint equations hold is an immediate consequence
of Lemmas
80
and
82
.
∎

6.
Proof of the improvements in the case of Gaussian foliations

The first step in the proof of Theorem
35
is to relate
\theta
and
t
.

Lemma 83
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
35
to be fulfilled. Then, using the notation of
Theorem
35
, there is, for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant
C_{l}
such that

(120)

\|\ln\theta(\cdot,t)+\ln t\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}+\|t\theta(\cdot,t)-1\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}\leq C_{l}t^{\varepsilon}

for all
t\leq t_{0}
. In particular, (
30
), (
16
) and (
18b
) can be
reformulated in terms of the mean curvature: there are smooth functions
\hat{b}_{AB}
,
A,B=1,\dots,n
, and, for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, a constant

C_{l}
such that

(121)

\displaystyle g=

\displaystyle-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{A,B}\hat{b}_{AB}\theta^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}}\mathscr{Y}^{A}\otimes\mathscr{Y}^{B},

(122)

\displaystyle\hat{h}:=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum}_{A,B}\hat{b}_{AB}\mathscr{Y}^{A}\otimes\mathscr{Y}^{B},

(123)

\displaystyle|\bar{D}^{l}(\hat{h}-\bar{h})|_{\bar{h}}\leq

\displaystyle C_{l}\theta^{-\varepsilon},

where the last estimate holds on
M_{0}
.

Remark 84
.

Given (
120
), the last statement of the lemma can be reversed. In other words, given (
120
),
(
121
), (
122
) and (
123
), it is possible to deduce that
(
30
), (
16
) and (
18b
) hold.

Proof.

Due to the Hamiltonian constraint,
[
69
, (8), p. 7]
, the equality (
118
) holds. On the other hand, the
deceleration parameter
q
is given by

(124)

q=n-1-\frac{n^{2}}{n-1}\frac{\rho-\bar{p}}{\theta^{2}}-\frac{2n^{2}}{n-1}\frac{\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}+n\frac{\bar{S}}{\theta^{2}},

where we appealed to
[
69
, (266), p. 65]
and
\bar{p}:=\bar{g}^{ij}T_{ij}/n
, where
T
is the stress energy tensor associated with the scalar
field matter. Combining (
118
), (
124
),
[
69
, (151), p. 49]
and
[
69
, Remark 81, p. 49]

yields

(125)

q-(n-1)=-\frac{2n}{n-1}\frac{\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}+n[(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}-1].

Next, note that since
\Phi_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}=1
,

(126)

(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}-1=(\hat{U}\phi-\Phi_{1})^{2}+2\Phi_{1}(\hat{U}\phi-\Phi_{1})+\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{K}-\mathscr{K})^{2}+2\mathrm{tr}[\mathscr{K}(\mathcal{K}-\mathscr{K})].

In particular, for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C_{l}
such that

\|[(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}-1](\cdot,t)\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}\leq C_{l}t^{\varepsilon}

for
t\leq t_{0}
, due to the assumptions. Next, note that

(127)

\hat{U}(\varrho+\ln\theta)=-\frac{1}{n}[q-(n-1)]=\frac{2}{n-1}\frac{\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}+1-(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}-\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2},

where we appealed to
[
68
, (3.4), p. 26]
,
[
68
, (7.9), p. 74]
and (
125
). Let
\gamma
be an integral curve of
\hat{U}

such that
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
; cf. (
85
) and the adjacent text. Then, if
\Lambda\leq 0
,

\frac{d}{ds}[(\varrho+\ln\theta)\circ\gamma](s)\leq Ce^{\varepsilon s},

where we appealed to (
80
) and the fact that
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
. Integrating this estimate from
s_{a}
to
s_{b}\leq 0
,
where
s_{a}\leq s_{b}
and
t[\gamma(s_{b})]\leq t_{0}
, yields

s_{b}+\ln\theta\circ\gamma(s_{b})-s_{a}-\ln\theta\circ\gamma(s_{a})\leq Ce^{\varepsilon s_{b}}.

Assume now that
\gamma(s_{b})=(\bar{x},t_{0})
and
\gamma(s_{a})=(\bar{x},t)
with
0<t\leq t_{0}
. Then this inequality yields

\ln\theta(\bar{x},t)\geq-\varrho(\bar{x},t)+\varrho(\bar{x},t_{0})+\ln\theta(\bar{x},t_{0})-Ce^{\varepsilon\varrho(\bar{x},t_{0})}.

Since this is true for any
\bar{x}\in\bar{M}
and since (
80
) holds,

\ln\theta(\bar{x},t)\geq-\ln t+\ln\theta_{-}-C,

where
C
is allowed to depend on
t_{0}
, but not on
\bar{x}
. Moreover,
\theta_{-}:=\inf_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}\theta(\bar{x},t_{0})
. Since
\theta_{-}>0
by
assumption, we conclude that
\ln\theta\geq-\ln t-C
on
M_{0}
. Combining this observation with (
127
) yields the
conclusion that
\varrho+\ln\theta
is bounded, and thereby the conclusion that
\ln\theta+\ln t
is bounded. In other words, there is a
constant
C
such that

(128)

\|[\ln\theta+\varrho](\cdot,t)\|_{C^{0}(\bar{M})}+\|\ln\theta(\cdot,t)+\ln t\|_{C^{0}(\bar{M})}\leq C

for
t\leq t_{0}
.

If
\Lambda>0
, we assume that
\theta\geq\theta_{0}
on
M_{0}
, where
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\theta_{0}>[2\Lambda/(n-1)]^{1/2}
.
Then (
127
) can be used to deduce that
\ln\theta
tends to infinity
as
-\sigma\ln t
for some
\sigma>0
. Inserting this information into (
127
) again leads to the conclusion
that (
128
) holds.

Next, we wish to estimate
E_{\mathbf{I}}\ln\theta
; cf. the notation introduced at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
62
.
Apply, to this end,
E_{i}
to (
127
). This yields, recalling that
\hat{U}=\theta^{-1}\partial_{t}
in the present setting,

(129)

\begin{split}&-E_{i}(\ln\theta+\varrho)\hat{U}(\varrho+\ln\theta)+E_{i}(\varrho)\hat{U}(\varrho+\ln\theta)+\hat{U}[E_{i}(\varrho+\ln\theta)]\\
=&-\frac{4}{n-1}\frac{\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}E_{i}(\varrho+\ln\theta)+\frac{4}{n-1}\frac{\Lambda}{\theta^{2}}E_{i}(\varrho)+E_{i}[1-(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}-\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}].\end{split}

Introducing the notation
f:=E_{i}(\ln\theta+\varrho)
, this equality can schematically be written

\hat{U}(f)=g_{1}f+g_{2}.

Here
\|g_{i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{0}(\bar{M})}\leq Ct^{\varepsilon}
, where we appealed to (
80
), (
126
) and the fact that the
right hand side of (
127
) is
O(t^{\varepsilon})
. Evaluating this equality on an integral curve
\gamma
as above yields

\frac{d}{ds}(f^{2}\circ\gamma+1)\geq-2(|g_{1}\circ\gamma|+|g_{2}\circ\gamma|)(f^{2}\circ\gamma+1).

Since
g_{i}\circ\gamma(s)=O(e^{\varepsilon s})
, this estimate can be integrated to yield the conclusion that
f
is bounded on
M_{0}
. This means that
(
128
) can be improved in that the
C^{0}
-norm can be replaced by the
C^{1}
-norm. Proceeding inductively, and applying
successively higher order derivatives to (
129
), similar arguments yield the conclusion that for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there
is a constant
C_{l}
such that

(130)

\|[\ln\theta+\varrho](\cdot,t)\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}+\|\ln\theta(\cdot,t)+\ln t\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}\leq C

for all
t\leq t_{0}
.

Next, consider
\partial_{t}\theta^{-1}=-\theta^{-2}\partial_{t}\theta=(q+1)/n
. Integrating this equality from
0
to
t
, keeping in mind that
\theta^{-1}

vanishes at
t=0
, yields

[\theta(\bar{x},t)]^{-1}=t+\int_{0}^{t}[q(\bar{x},s)-(n-1)]/n\ ds.

Due to (
125
), (
130
) and the assumptions, this equality can be differentiated
l
times in order
to deduce that

\|\theta^{-1}(\cdot,t)-t\|_{C^{l}(\bar{M})}\leq Ct^{1+\varepsilon}

for
t\leq t_{0}
. The lemma follows.
∎

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
35

Finally, we are in a position to prove Theorem
35
.

Theorem
35
.

The idea is to verify that the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied and to combine the
conclusions of this theorem with an estimate relating
t
and
\theta
in order to obtain the desired conclusions. Due to
Lemma
83
, we know that (
120
) holds and that the metric can be represented by
(
121
). This means, in particular, that the metric can be represented as in (
24
)
with
N=1
. Due to (
120
),
\theta
diverges to
\infty
uniformly. Moreover, due to (
18a
), (
123
)
and (
120
), it follows that (
20a
) and (
20b
) hold. Next, due to
(
120
) and the fact that
\hat{N}=\theta
, it follows that (
25
) holds and that the first term on the
left hand side of (
26
) is bounded by a third times the right hand side. Since the third term on the left hand
side vanishes identically (since
N=1
), it remains to estimate the middle term. On the other hand,

t\partial_{t}(\phi-\Phi_{1}\ln t)=t\theta\hat{U}(\phi)-\Phi_{1}=t\theta[\hat{U}(\phi)-\Phi_{1}]+(t\theta-1)\Phi_{1}.

The first term on the right hand side is bounded by
C_{k}t^{\varepsilon}
in
C^{k}
and the second term on the right hand side is bounded by

C_{k}t^{\varepsilon}
in
C^{k}
due to (
120
). Integrating the corresponding estimate results in the existence of a

\Phi_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M})
such that

\|\phi(\cdot,t)-\Phi_{1}\ln t-\Phi_{0}\|_{C^{k}(\bar{M})}\leq C_{k}t^{\varepsilon}

for all
t\leq t_{0}
.
This estimate implies that the second term on the right hand side of (
26
) satisfies the desired bound. To conclude,
all the conditions of Theorem
28
are satisfied. This means that all the conclusions hold, and due to
(
120
), the mean curvature
\theta
can be replaced by
t^{-1}
in the conclusions. This yields the conclusions of
Theorem
35
.
∎

7.
Obtaining data on the singularity from convergent solutions

For the remainder of the article, we assume that we have a solution which converges in the sense described at the beginning of
Subsection
1.7
. The goal is then to conclude that such solutions yield data on the singularity. We
begin by deducing information concerning the asymptotics of the expansion normalised Weingarten map in the
C^{0}
-setting.

7.1.
Asymptotics in
C^{0}

Let us begin by deriving limits for
\mathcal{K}
and
\hat{U}\phi
.

Lemma 85
.

Let
(M,g,\phi)
be a solution to the Einstein-scalar field equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda
and a uniform volume singularity
at
t=0
; cf. Definition
39
. If (
33
) holds with
k=0
, there is a continuous
(1,1)
-tensor field
\mathscr{K}

on
\bar{M}
and a constant
C
, depending only on
C_{0}
,
a_{0}
,
n
and
\epsilon_{0}
, such that

(131)

|\mathcal{K}-\mathscr{K}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. If, in addition, (
34
) holds, there is, analogously, a continuous function
\Phi_{1}
on
\bar{M}
such that

(132)

|\hat{U}\phi-\Phi_{1}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
.

Proof.

Let, to begin with,
\{E_{i}\}
be a local orthonormal frame on
T\bar{M}
with respect to
\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}
, and let
\{\omega^{i}\}
be the dual frame. Let,
moreover,
\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j}:=\mathcal{K}(\omega^{i},E_{j})
. Due to
[
68
, (A.4), p. 204]
and (
33
),

(133)

|\hat{U}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})|=|(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\omega^{i},E_{j})|\leq C_{0}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. Let
\gamma
be an integral curve with respect to
\hat{U}
. Then, due to
[
68
, (7.9), p. 74]
,

\frac{d}{ds}(\varrho\circ\gamma)(s)=[\hat{U}(\varrho)]\circ\gamma(s)=1.

In particular, we can parametrise
\gamma
so that
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
. Combining this observation with (
133
) yields

\left|\frac{d}{ds}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j}\circ\gamma)(s)\right|\leq C_{0}\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon s}

for all
s
in the domain of definition of
\gamma
; note that the
\bar{M}
-component of
\gamma
is independent of
s
, so that
\epsilon
is evaluated at a fixed
point of
\bar{M}
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}\circ\gamma
converges to a limit and there is a
\mathscr{K}
and a constant

C
, depending only on
C_{0}
,
a_{0}
,
n
and
\epsilon_{0}
, such that (
131
) holds. Since
\varrho
converges to
-\infty
uniformly,
(
131
) implies that
\mathcal{K}(\cdot,t)
converges uniformly to
\mathscr{K}
, so that
\mathscr{K}
has to be continuous.

The proof of (
132
) is similar.
∎

Let
p_{A}
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
. Since the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}
are real, and the
p_{A}
are limits of the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}
,
the
p_{A}
are real. In what follows, we assume the
p_{A}
to be distinct and order them so that
p_{1}<\cdots<p_{n}
. Then

(134)

|\ell_{A}-p_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
, where
\ell_{A}
denotes the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}
. Since the convergence is uniform, we can restrict the interval
I
in such a way
that the
\ell_{A}
are distinct on
M
. In fact, the following holds.

Lemma 86
.

Let
(M,g,\phi)
be a solution to the Einstein-scalar field equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda
and a
uniform volume singularity at
t=0
. Assume that (
33
) holds with
k=0
. Assume, moreover,
that the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
(whose existence is ensured by Lemma
85
) are distinct. Then, by restricting the
interval
I
, if necessary, it can be assumed that the eigenvalues
\ell_{A}
of
\mathcal{K}
are distinct on
M
and that there is a constant

\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}>0
such that the difference between the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}
is bounded from below by
\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}
on
M
. Moreover, by taking
a finite covering space of
\bar{M}
, if necessary, there is, for each
\ell_{A}
, a corresponding global eigenvector field
X_{A}
of
\mathcal{K}

with
|X_{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}=1
. Let
\{Y^{A}\}
be the frame dual to
\{X_{A}\}
. Then there is a frame
\{\mathscr{X}_{A}\}
of continuous eigenvector
fields of
\mathscr{K}
, with dual frame
\{\mathscr{Y}^{A}\}
and a constant
C
such that

(135)

|X_{A}-\mathscr{X}_{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|Y^{A}-\mathscr{Y}^{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
.

Remark 87
.

In what follows, we assume the conditions of this lemma to be satisfied. Moreover, we also restrict
I
as in the lemma, and replace
\bar{M}

with an appropriate finite cover. We also order the
\ell_{A}
so that
\ell_{1}<\cdots<\ell_{n}
. Note also that the
X_{A}
are uniquely defined
up to a sign (we here assume
\bar{M}
to be connected).

Proof.

Since
\bar{M}
is compact and the
p_{A}
are continuous functions on
\bar{M}
, there is an
\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}>0
such that

\min_{A\neq B}|p_{A}-p_{B}|\geq 2\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}
on
\bar{M}
. Since (
134
) holds, where the convergence is uniform, we can
ensure that
|\ell_{A}(\cdot,t)-p_{A}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}/2
for
t\in I
by restricting
I
, if necessary. Then
\min_{A\neq B}|\ell_{A}-\ell_{B}|\geq\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}

on
M
. Appealing to
[
68
, Lemma A.1, p. 201]
, we can, by taking a finite covering space of
\bar{M}
, if necessary, assume that there is a
global frame, say
\{X_{A}\}
, of eigenvector fields of
\mathcal{K}
, with
\mathcal{K}X_{A}=\ell_{A}X_{A}
(no summation), and
|X_{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}=1
. The remaining
statement of the lemma is an immediate consequence of the previous observations and the fact that (
131
) holds.
∎

This lemma justifies the introduction of the standard assumptions; cf. Definition
40
. Next, define
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}
by the
condition that

(136)

\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}X_{A}=\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}X_{B}+\overline{\mathcal{W}}^{0}_{A}U;

cf.
[
68
, (6.5), p. 68]
. Here
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}=\theta^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}
.

Lemma 88
.

Assume the standard assumptions to hold, cf. Definition
40
. Define
\bar{\mu}_{A}
by the condition that

|X_{A}|_{\bar{g}}=e^{\bar{\mu}_{A}}
. Then there is a constant
C
and a continuous function
r_{A}
on
\bar{M}
such that

(137)

|\bar{\mu}_{A}-p_{A}\varrho-r_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
.

Proof.

Combining (
33
) for
k=0
with
[
68
, (6.7)–(6.8), p. 68]
, the fact that
\min_{A\neq B}|\ell_{A}-\ell_{B}|\geq\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}

on
M
, the fact that
|X_{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}=1
and the fact that
|Y^{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}
is bounded (this follows from
[
68
, Lemma 5.5, p. 60]
)
implies that
|\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}
on
M
. Combining this observation with

[
68
, (7.8)–(7.9), p. 74]
yields

(138)

\hat{U}(\bar{\mu}_{A}-p_{A}\varrho)=\ell_{A}+\mathcal{W}^{A}_{A}-p_{A}=O(\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho})

on
M
, where we appealed to (
134
). At this point, we can argue as in the proof of Lemma
85

in order to conclude that
\bar{\mu}_{A}-p_{A}\varrho
converges exponentially to a continuous function
r_{A}
on
\bar{M}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we estimate
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}
.

Lemma 89
.

Assume the standard assumptions to be satisfied and let
\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}
be defined by (
136
). Then there is a constant
C
such that

(139)

|\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
.

Proof.

By the argument presented at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
88
,
|\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}
on
M
.
Next, for
A\neq B
, the right hand side of
[
68
, (6.19), p. 70]
is antisymmetric. Thus

(140)

|\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|\leq e^{-2(\bar{\mu}_{A}-\bar{\mu}_{B})}|\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A}+\epsilon)\varrho}

on
M
, where we appealed to (
137
). The lemma follows.
∎

Next, let
X_{A}^{B}
and
Y_{A}^{B}
be defined as in Definition
42
. It is of interest to estimate the asymptotic behaviour of

X_{A}^{B}
and
Y^{A}_{B}
. Due to (
135
), we already know that there is a constant
C
such that

(141)

|X_{A}^{B}-\delta_{A}^{B}|+|Y_{A}^{B}-\delta_{A}^{B}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. However, these estimates can be improved.

Lemma 90
.

Assume the standard assumptions to be satisfied. Then there are constants
a
and
C
such that

(142)

|X_{B}^{A}-\delta_{B}^{A}|+|Y_{B}^{A}-\delta_{B}^{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that (
136
) yields

(143)

\hat{U}(X_{A}^{B})\mathscr{X}_{B}+X_{A}(\ln N)\hat{U}=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}X_{B}^{C}\mathscr{X}_{C}+\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{A}^{0}U.

This equality requires a comment, since the vector fields
\mathscr{X}_{A}
can, at this stage, only be assumed to be continuous. This means that

X_{A}^{B}
can only be assumed to be continuous. On the other hand, the
X_{A}^{B}
are smooth considered as functions of
t
. For this reason,
the time derivative of
X_{A}^{B}
is well defined.

One particular consequence of (
143
) is that
\hat{U}(X_{A}^{C})=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}X_{B}^{C}
. The goal is to use this equation in order to
improve our knowledge concerning the asymptotic behaviour of the
X_{A}
. As a first step, note that (
141
) holds. Let
\gamma
be
an integral curve of
\hat{U}
with
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
, cf. the proof of Lemma
85
. Then, if
A\geq 2
,

(144)

\frac{d}{ds}X_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma\cdot X_{1}^{1}\circ\gamma+\textstyle{\sum}_{B\geq 2}\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}\circ\gamma\cdot X_{B}^{1}\circ\gamma.

The corresponding
n-1
equations can be written

\dot{\xi}=M\xi+\zeta,

where
\xi
is the vector consisting of the
n-1
elements
X_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma
,
A=2,\dots,n
;
\zeta
is the vector consisting of the
n-1

elements
\mathcal{W}_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma\cdot X_{1}^{1}\circ\gamma
,
A=2,\dots,n
;
\|M(s)\|\leq C\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon s}
;

|\xi(s)|\leq C\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2\epsilon s}
; and
|\zeta(s)|\leq C\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{2}-p_{1}+\epsilon)s}
; these estimates are immediate consequences of
(
139
) and (
141
), and the constants (in these estimates and the ones below) are independent of
\gamma
.
Then, for
-\infty<s_{a}\leq s\leq 0
in the domain of definition of
\gamma
,

\xi(s)=\Phi(s;s_{a})\xi(s_{a})+\int_{s_{a}}^{s}\Phi(s;\tau)\zeta(\tau)d\tau,

where
\Phi(s;s_{a})
is defined by
\dot{\Phi}(s;s_{a})=M(s)\Phi(s;s_{a})
and
\Phi(s_{a};s_{a})=\mathrm{Id}
. Due to the bounds on
\|M(s)\|
,
there is a constant
C
such that
\|\Phi(s;s_{a})\|\leq C
for all
-\infty<s_{a}\leq s\leq 0
in the domain of definition of
\gamma
. This implies,
in particular, that

|\xi(s)|\leq C|\xi(s_{a})|+C\int_{s_{a}}^{s}|\zeta(\tau)|d\tau.

Letting
s_{a}\rightarrow-\infty
in this estimate, keeping in mind that
|\zeta(s)|\leq C\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{2}-p_{1}+\epsilon)s}
, yields

(145)

|\xi(s)|\leq C\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{2}-p_{1}+\epsilon)s}.

Next, assume inductively that for some
2\leq m\leq n-1
, there are constants
a
and
C
such that

(146)

|X^{1}_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\max\{e^{2(p_{A}-p_{1})\varrho},e^{2(p_{m}-p_{1})\varrho}\}

for all
A\geq 2
. Note that we know this inductive assumption to hold with
m=2
. Then, if
A>m
,

(147)

\frac{d}{ds}X_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma=\textstyle{\sum}_{B=1}^{m}\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}\circ\gamma\cdot X_{B}^{1}\circ\gamma+\textstyle{\sum}_{B>m}\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}\circ\gamma\cdot X_{B}^{1}\circ\gamma.

Due to (
139
) and (
146
), the first sum on the right hand side is
O(\langle s\rangle^{a}e^{2(p_{A}-p_{1}+\epsilon)s})
.
Viewing (
147
) as an equation for
X_{A}^{1}\circ\gamma
for
A>m
, we can argue as above in order to conclude that
the inductive hypothesis holds with
m
replaced by
m+1
. To conclude, there are constants
a
and
C
such that

(148)

|X^{1}_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}e^{2(p_{A}-p_{1})\varrho}

for all
A\geq 2
.

As a next inductive step, assume that for some
1\leq m\leq n-2
, there are constants
a
and
C
such that

(149)

|X^{A}_{B}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}

for all
B>A
such that
A\leq m
. We know this to be true for
m=1
. Next, for
A>m+1
,

\frac{d}{ds}X^{m+1}_{A}\circ\gamma=\textstyle{\sum}_{B\leq m+1}\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}\circ\gamma\cdot X^{m+1}_{B}\circ\gamma+\textstyle{\sum}_{B>m+1}\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}\circ\gamma\cdot X^{m+1}_{B}\circ\gamma.

The first sum on the right hand side is
O(\langle s\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{A}-p_{m+1})s})
. We can thus argue as before in order to conclude that

|X^{m+1}_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{0}}e^{2(p_{m+2}-p_{m+1}+\epsilon)\varrho}

on
M
for all
A>m+1
. Assume now, inductively, that there is a
k
, satisfying
m+1<k\leq n-1
, and constants
a
and
C
such that

(150)

|X^{m+1}_{A}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}\max\{e^{2(p_{k}-p_{m+1}+\epsilon)\varrho},e^{2(p_{A}-p_{m+1}+\epsilon)\varrho}\}

on
M
for all
A>m+1
. We know this to be true for
k=m+2
. If
A>k
,

\frac{d}{ds}X^{m+1}_{A}\circ\gamma=\textstyle{\sum}_{B\leq k}\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}\circ\gamma\cdot X^{m+1}_{B}\circ\gamma+\textstyle{\sum}_{B>k}\mathcal{W}^{B}_{A}\circ\gamma\cdot X^{m+1}_{B}\circ\gamma.

Moreover, the first term on the right hand side is
O(\langle s\rangle^{a}e^{2(p_{A}-p_{m+1}+\epsilon)})
and we can argue as before in order to prove that

k
can be replaced by
k+1
in the inductive hypothesis (
150
). This means that we can replace
m
by
m+1
in
(
149
). Thus (
149
) holds for all
B>A
. In particular, the first term on the
left hand side of (
142
) is bounded by the right hand side.

Finally, note that
Y^{A}_{B}
are the components of the inverse of the matrix with components
X^{A}_{B}
. Combining this observation with
the estimates for
X^{A}_{B}
and an argument similar to the one presented at the end of the proof of Lemma
65
yields the
conclusion that the second term on the left hand side of (
142
) satisfies the desired bound.
∎

At this stage, we can derive asymptotics for the metric,
\mathcal{K}
and
\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}
. In other words, we can prove Theorem
43
.

Theorem
43
.

Note, to begin with, that

(151)

g=-N^{2}dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{2\bar{\mu}_{A}}Y^{A}\otimes Y^{A}.

In particular,

g(\mathscr{X}_{B},\mathscr{X}_{C})=\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{2\bar{\mu}_{A}}Y^{A}(\mathscr{X}_{B})Y^{A}(\mathscr{X}_{C})=\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{2\bar{\mu}_{A}}Y^{A}_{B}Y^{A}_{C}.

Combining this observation with (
137
) and (
142
) yields, if
A\neq B
,

e^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}\varrho}|g(\mathscr{X}_{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
(no summation). Moreover, if
r_{A}
are the functions whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
88
,

|e^{-2p_{A}\varrho-2r_{A}}g(\mathscr{X}_{A},\mathscr{X}_{A})-1|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
(no summation). Due to these observations, it is clear that (
35
) and (
36
) hold. That
(
37a
) and (
37b
) hold follows immediately from (
137
)
and (
142
) respectively.

Next, note that

(152)

\mathcal{K}=\textstyle{\sum}_{A}\ell_{A}X_{A}\otimes Y^{A}.

Thus

(153)

\mathcal{K}(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}\ell_{C}\mathscr{Y}^{A}(X_{C})Y^{C}(\mathscr{X}_{B})=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}\ell_{C}X^{A}_{C}Y^{C}_{B}.

Combining this equality with (
134
) and (
142
) yields the conclusion that
(
37c
) holds. Finally, note that

(154)

(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\mathscr{Y}^{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})=(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(X^{A}_{C}Y^{C},Y^{D}_{B}X_{D})=X^{A}_{C}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(Y^{C},X_{D})Y^{D}_{B}.

If
A\geq B
, then (
37d
) is an immediate consequence of the fact that (
33
) holds with
k=0
.
The interesting case is thus
A<B
. If
C=D
, then the estimate of the far right hand side of (
154
) is
similar to the proof of (
37c
) for
A<B
. If
C\neq D
, the term that needs to be estimated is

(\ell_{D}-\ell_{C})X^{A}_{C}\mathcal{W}^{C}_{D}Y^{D}_{B},

where we appealed to
[
68
, (6.8), p. 68]
. Combining this observation with (
139
) and (
142
)
yields (
37d
). The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we derive conclusions concerning the mean curvature, assuming (
34
) to hold for
k=0
and the limit, say
\Phi_{1}
,
of
\hat{U}\phi
to satisfy
\Phi_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}=1
.

Lemma 91
.

Assume the standard assumptions to be satisfied. Assume, moreover, that (
34
) holds for
k=0
and that the limit, say

\Phi_{1}
, of
\hat{U}\phi
satisfies
\Phi_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}=1
. Then there are constants
a
and
C
and a continuous function
r_{\theta}
such that

|\ln\theta+\varrho-r_{\theta}|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
.

Proof.

Since the proof is very similar to the beginning of the proof of Lemma
83
, combined with arguments similar
to the proof of Lemma
85
, we leave the details to the reader.
∎

7.2.
Higher order derivatives

In the previous subsection, we derive conclusions in
C^{0}
. In the present subsection, we derive estimates in
C^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
. In order to
be able to do so, we need to impose additional assumptions; cf. Definition
45
.

Lemma 92
.

Let
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and assume the
k
-standard assumptions to hold; cf. Definition
45
. Then the tensor field
\mathscr{K}
whose
existence is guaranteed by Lemma
85
is
C^{k}
. Moreover, there are constants
a_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(155)

\textstyle{\sum}_{l=0}^{k}|\bar{D}^{l}(\mathcal{K}-\mathscr{K})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. In particular, if
\ell_{A}
,
X_{A}
,
Y^{A}
,
\mathscr{X}_{A}
and
\mathscr{Y}^{A}
are given by Definition
86
and
p_{A}

are the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
, ordered by size, then
\mathscr{X}_{A}
,
\mathscr{Y}^{A}
and
p_{A}
are
C^{k}
and there are constants
a_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(156)

|\bar{D}^{l}(\ell_{A}-p_{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|\bar{D}^{l}(X_{A}-\mathscr{X}_{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|\bar{D}^{l}(Y^{A}-\mathscr{Y}^{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for all
0\leq l\leq k
and all
A\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Proof.

Let
\{E_{i}\}
be a global orthonormal frame with respect to
\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}
and let
\{\omega^{i}\}
be the dual frame. If
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
,
where
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, then we use the notation
\bar{D}_{\mathbf{I}}:=\bar{D}_{E_{i_{1}}}\dots\bar{D}_{E_{i_{m}}}
,
E_{\mathbf{I}}:=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{m}}
and
|\mathbf{I}|=m
.
One can either check directly or verify by appealing to, e.g.,
[
68
, Lemma 5.7, p. 62]
, that if
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
, then

(157)

|\bar{D}_{\mathbf{I}}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
, where we appealed to (
33
). Next, we wish to prove that for
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
,

(158)

|E_{\mathbf{I}}[\hat{U}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})]|\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
, where
\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j}=\mathcal{K}(\omega^{i},E_{j})
. Note, to this end, that, due to
[
68
, (A.4), p. 204]
,

\hat{U}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})=(\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\omega^{i},E_{j}).

It can therefore be demonstrated inductively that
E_{\mathbf{I}}[\hat{U}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})]
consists of a linear combination of terms of the form

(\bar{D}_{\mathbf{J}}\hat{\mathcal{L}}_{U}\mathcal{K})(\bar{D}_{\mathbf{K}}\omega^{i},\bar{D}_{\mathbf{L}}E_{j}),

where
|\mathbf{J}|+|\mathbf{K}|+|\mathbf{L}|=|\mathbf{I}|
. Combining this observation with (
157
) yields the conclusion that
(
158
) holds. Next, we wish to commute
E_{\mathbf{I}}
and
\hat{U}
. Note, to this end, that
[E_{\mathbf{I}},\hat{U}]
can be written
as a linear combination of terms of the form

(159)

E_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}(\ln\hat{N})\cdots E_{\mathbf{I}_{m}}(\ln\hat{N})E_{\mathbf{J}}\hat{U},

where
|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+\dots+|\mathbf{I}_{m}|+|\mathbf{J}|=|\mathbf{I}|
,
|\mathbf{I}_{j}|\neq 0
and
|\mathbf{J}|<|\mathbf{I}|
. Combining this observation with (
158
)
and the assumptions yields the conclusion that if
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
, then

|\hat{U}[E_{\mathbf{I}}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})]|\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. At this stage, we can argue as in the proof of Lemma
85
in order to conclude that
E_{\mathbf{I}}(\mathcal{K}^{i}_{j})
converges
at the rate
\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}
. Since the convergence is uniform, we conclude that the
\mathscr{K}
whose existence is guaranteed by
Lemma
85
is in fact
C^{k}
. Moreover, we obtain (
155
). The estimate (
156
) is
an immediate consequence of this.
∎

Next, we derive information concerning the asymptotics of the scalar field, given estimates of the form (
34
).

Lemma 93
.

Assume that the standard assumptions hold; cf. Definition
40
. Assume, moreover, that
(
34
) holds for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
k\geq 1
, assume, in addition, that (
38
) holds. Then
there are
C^{k}
-functions
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}
on
\bar{M}
and constants
a_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(160)

|\bar{D}^{l}[\hat{U}(\phi)-\Phi_{1}]|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|\bar{D}^{l}(\phi-\Phi_{1}\varrho-\Phi_{0})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for all
0\leq l\leq k
.

Remark 94
.

In order to derive conclusions concerning the scalar field, another possibility is to impose assumptions on the geometry and to deduce conclusions
concerning the scalar field; cf. the proof of Lemma
80
. We encourage the interested reader to provide the
details.

Proof.

The proof of the fact that
\hat{U}\phi
converges to a
C^{k}
-function
\Phi_{1}
and the fact that the first term on the left hand side of
(
160
) is bounded by the right hand side is very similar to the proof of Lemma
92
.
We therefore omit the details. On the other hand, since (
116
) holds, the proof of the existence of
\Phi_{0}
and
the fact that the second term on the left hand side of (
160
) is bounded by the right hand side is identical.
The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we prove that the functions
r_{A}
whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
88
are
C^{k}
.

Lemma 95
.

Let
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and assume the
k
-standard assumptions to hold; cf. Definition
45
. Then the functions
r_{A}
whose
existence is guaranteed by Lemma
88
are
C^{k}
. Moreover, there are constants
a_{k}
and
C_{k}
such that

(161)

|\bar{D}^{l}(\bar{\mu}_{A}-p_{A}\varrho-r_{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for all
A\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
0\leq l\leq k
.

Proof.

Consider, to begin with,
[
68
, (6.7)–(6.8), p. 68]
. Combining these formulae with the conclusions of Lemma
92
and
the assumptions yields the conclusion that

|\bar{D}^{l}\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for
0\leq l\leq k
and
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Combining this with the first equality in (
138
)
and (
156
) yields the conclusion that

|E_{\mathbf{I}}\hat{U}(\bar{\mu}_{A}-p_{A}\varrho)|\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for
0\leq l\leq k
and
A\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Due to the observations made in the proof of Lemma
92
, we can commute

E_{\mathbf{I}}
and
\hat{U}
. Integrating the resulting estimate as in the proof of Lemma
88
yields the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

In what follows, it is of interest to estimate the spatial derivatives of
\bar{\mu}_{A}
and
\mu_{A}
.

Lemma 96
.

Let
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and assume the
k
-standard assumptions to hold; cf. Definition
45
. Then there are constants

C_{k}
and
a_{k}
such that

(162)

|\bar{D}^{l}\varrho|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|\bar{D}^{l}\bar{\mu}_{A}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}

on
M
for all
0\leq l\leq k
and
A\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Assume, in addition to the above, that (
34
) holds and that the limit
\Phi_{1}
, whose existence is guaranteed by
Lemma
93
, satisfies
\Phi_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}=1
. Then the function
r_{\theta}
, whose existence is guaranteed
by Lemma
91
, satisfies
r_{\theta}\in C^{k}(\bar{M})
. Moreover, there are constants
L_{k}
and
b_{k}
such that

(163)

\textstyle{\sum}_{l=0}^{k}|\bar{D}^{l}(\ln\theta+\varrho-r_{\theta})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq L_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{b_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
. In particular, there are constants
M_{k}
and
c_{k}
such that

(164)

\textstyle{\sum}_{l=0}^{k}|\bar{D}^{l}(\mu_{A}-(p_{A}-1)\varrho-r_{A}-r_{\theta})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq M_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{c_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for all
A\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Proof.

Due to
[
68
, (7.9), p. 74]
,
\hat{U}(\varrho)=1
. Thus, for
\mathbf{I}\neq 0
,

\hat{U}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\varrho)=[\hat{U},E_{\mathbf{I}}]\varrho+E_{\mathbf{I}}\hat{U}\varrho=[\hat{U},E_{\mathbf{I}}]\varrho.

However, the right hand side consists of a sum of terms of the form (
159
) applied to
\varrho
, where

|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+\dots+|\mathbf{I}_{m}|+|\mathbf{J}|=|\mathbf{I}|
and
|\mathbf{I}_{j}|\neq 0
; cf. the proof of Lemma
92
. Since
E_{\mathbf{J}}\hat{U}\varrho

equals zero if
\mathbf{J}\neq 0
and equals
1
if
\mathbf{J}=0
,
\hat{U}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\varrho)
does not grow faster than polynomially in
\varrho
. In particular, if

\gamma
is an integral curve of
\hat{U}
with
\varrho\circ\gamma(s)=s
, then

\frac{d}{ds}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\varrho)\circ\gamma=f_{\mathbf{I}}(s),

where
|f_{\mathbf{I}}(s)|\leq C_{k}\langle s\rangle^{a_{k}}
on the domain of definition of
\gamma
, assuming
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
, and
C_{k}
is a constant independent of
\gamma
.
In particular, it is clear that the first term on the left hand side of (
162
) is bounded by the right hand side. Combining
this estimate with (
161
) yields the conclusion that (
162
) holds.

The proof of (
163
) is similar to the proof of Lemma
83
, and we omit the
details. Finally, (
164
) is an immediate consequence of (
161
) and (
163
),
keeping the fact that
\mu_{A}=\bar{\mu}_{A}+\ln\theta
in mind; cf. the comments made in connection with
[
68
, (3.10)–(3.11), p. 29]
.
∎

At this stage, we can derive a higher order analogue of Lemma
89
.

Lemma 97
.

Let
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and assume the
k
-standard assumptions to hold; cf. Definition
45
. Then there are constants

C_{k}
and
a_{k}
such that

(165)

|\bar{D}^{l}\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
for all
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and all
0\leq l\leq k
.

Proof.

Due to
[
68
, (6.7)–(6.8), p. 68]
, (
156
) and (
33
), it is clear that

(166)

|\bar{D}^{l}\mathcal{W}^{A}_{B}|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}

on
M
for all
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and all
0\leq l\leq k
. Thus (
165
) holds for
A\geq B
. In case

A<B
, (
107
) holds. Differentiating this equality, keeping (
137
), (
162
)
and (
166
) in mind, yields (
165
) for
A<B
.
∎

The next goal is to derive a higher order version of Lemma
90
.

Lemma 98
.

Let
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and assume the
k
-standard assumptions to hold; cf. Definition
45
. Then there are constants
C_{k}
and

a_{k}
such that

(167)

|\bar{D}^{l}(X_{B}^{A}-\delta_{B}^{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+|\bar{D}^{l}(Y_{B}^{A}-\delta_{B}^{A})|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{k}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
for all
0\leq l\leq k
and all
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that Lemma
92
implies that
X_{A}^{B}
is
C^{k}
with respect to the spatial variables and infinitely
differentiable with respect to
t
. Moreover, due to the proof of Lemma
90
,
X^{A}_{B}
satisfies the relation

\hat{U}(X_{A}^{C})=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}X_{B}^{C}
. Introducing
Z^{A}_{B}=X^{A}_{B}-\delta^{A}_{B}
, this relation can be written

(168)

\hat{U}(Z^{C}_{A})=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}Z_{B}^{C}+\mathcal{W}^{C}_{A}.

Let
0\leq m<k
and assume, inductively, that

(169)

|E_{\mathbf{I}}Z^{A}_{B}|\leq C_{m}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{m}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
for all
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
|\mathbf{I}|\leq m
. Note that if this estimate holds, then

(170)

|E_{\mathbf{I}}\hat{U}Z^{A}_{B}|\leq C_{m}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{m}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
for all
A,B\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
|\mathbf{I}|\leq m
. This is a consequence of combining (
169
) with
(
165
) and (
168
).

Due to Lemma
90
, we know that for
m=0
, the inductive hypothesis (
169
) holds.
Next, let
\mathbf{I}
be such that
|\mathbf{I}|=m+1
and commute the equation (
168
) with
E_{\mathbf{I}}
. This yields

(171)

\hat{U}(E_{\mathbf{I}}Z^{C}_{A})=[\hat{U},E_{\mathbf{I}}]Z^{C}_{A}+E_{\mathbf{I}}(\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}Z_{B}^{C})+E_{\mathbf{I}}(\mathcal{W}^{C}_{A}).

On the other hand,
[\hat{U},E_{\mathbf{I}}]
can be written as a linear combination of terms of the form (
159
) where

|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+\dots+|\mathbf{I}_{m}|+|\mathbf{J}|=|\mathbf{I}|
,
|\mathbf{I}_{j}|\neq 0
and
|\mathbf{J}|<|\mathbf{I}|
. Due to this fact, the assumptions, (
165
),
(
169
) and (
170
), the equation (
171
) can be rewritten

\hat{U}(E_{\mathbf{I}}Z^{C}_{A})=\mathcal{W}_{A}^{B}E_{\mathbf{I}}Z_{B}^{C}+F_{A}^{C},

where

|F_{B}^{A}|\leq C_{m}\langle\varrho\rangle^{a_{m}}e^{2\epsilon\varrho}\min\{1,e^{2(p_{B}-p_{A})\varrho}\}

on
M
. At this stage, an argument similar to the proof of Lemma
90
yields the conclusion that
(
169
) holds with
m
replaced by
m+1
. Thus (
169
) holds with
m=k
.
Thus the first term on the left hand side of (
167
) is bounded by the right hand side.

Finally, since the
Y^{A}_{B}
are the components of the inverse of the matrix with components
X^{A}_{B}
, an argument similar to the one
presented at the end of the proof of Lemma
65
yields the conclusion that the second term on the left hand side of
(
167
) satisfies the desired bound.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to prove Theorem
46
.

Theorem
46
.

Since
\{\mathscr{Y}^{A}\}
is a frame for the cotangent space for the leaves of the foliation, it is clear that the metric can be written in the
form (
39
). Moreover,

b_{AB}=e^{-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}\varrho}g(\mathscr{X}_{A},\mathscr{X}_{B})=\textstyle{\sum}_{C}e^{2\bar{\mu}_{C}-2p_{\max\{A,B\}}\varrho}Y^{C}_{A}Y^{C}_{B},

where we appealed to (
151
). Combining this equality with Lemma
95
and
(
167
) yields the conclusion that (
40
) holds.

The estimates (
41a
) and (
41b
) follow immediately from
(
161
) and (
167
) respectively. Next, note that (
152
) and
(
153
) hold. Combining these equalities with (
156
) and
(
167
) yields (
41c
). The proof of
(
41d
) is similar to the end of the proof of Theorem
43
, given the assumptions,
(
156
), (
165
) and (
167
).

The estimate (
42
) is an immediate consequence of (
160
) and
(
43
) is an immediate consequence of (
163
).
The equality (
44a
) and the estimate (
44b
) follow by combining
(
43
)
with (
39
) and (
40
). Next, the estimates (
46a
) and
(
46c
) follow from (
41a
) and
(
41b
) respectively. The estimate (
46b
) follows from
(
164
). Next, (
46d
) and (
46e
)
follow from (
163
), (
41c
) and (
41d
). The estimate
(
47
) follows from (
42
), (
125
), (
126
),
(
155
), (
162
) and (
163
).

Next, assume
N=1
and compute

(172)

\partial_{t}\theta^{-1}=-\theta^{-1}\partial_{t}\ln\theta=-n^{-1}\hat{U}(n\ln\theta)=n^{-1}(q+1)=1+n^{-1}[q-(n-1)].

Since the right hand side converges uniformly to
1
as
t\rightarrow 0+
, there is a
T>0
such that on
(0,T]
,
1/2\leq\partial_{t}\theta\leq 2

on
\bar{M}\times(0,T]
. Since
\theta\rightarrow\infty
uniformly as
t\rightarrow 0+
, we conclude that
t/2\leq\theta^{-1}\leq 2t
on
(0,T]
.
Combining this observation with (
172
) and (
47
) yields the
conclusion that there are constants
C_{k}
and
a_{k}
such that

|\bar{D}^{l}(\partial_{t}\theta^{-1}-1)|_{\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq C_{k}\langle\ln t\rangle^{a_{k}}t^{2\epsilon}

on
M
for
0\leq l\leq k
. Integrating this estimate yields (
48
). Combining
(
48
) with (
44a
) and (
44b
) yields
(
49a
) and (
49b
).
∎

7.3.
Reproducing the conditions on data on the singularity

Next, we prove Theorem
49
.

Theorem
49
.

Recall, to begin with, the Hamiltonian constraint (
118
). Next,

2\Omega+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}=(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{-2\mu_{A}}(X_{A}\phi)^{2};

see
[
69
, (151), p. 49]
. Combining these equalities yields

(173)

\theta^{-2}\bar{S}=(\hat{U}\phi)^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathcal{K}^{2}-1+\textstyle{\sum}_{A}e^{-2\mu_{A}}(X_{A}\phi)^{2}+2\Omega_{\Lambda}.

Our first goal is to prove that the right hand side of (
173
) converges to zero. Due to
Lemma
91
, it is clear that
\Omega_{\Lambda}
decays as
e^{2\varrho}
.
Due to (
135
), (
160
) and (
162
), we know that
X_{A}\phi
does not grow
faster than polynomially in
\varrho
. Next, note that since the sum of the
p_{A}^{2}
is bounded from above by
1
, since there is an

\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}>0
such that
|p_{A}-p_{B}|\geq\epsilon_{\mathrm{nd}}
, and since
n\geq 3
, there is an
\epsilon_{\mathrm{Sp}}>0
such that
p_{A}\leq 1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{Sp}}
. Combining this
observation with (
164
) yields the conclusion that there is a constant
C
such that

(174)

\mu_{A}\geq-\epsilon_{\mathrm{Sp}}\varrho-C.

Due to (
131
), (
160
), the fact that
\bar{\Phi}_{a}^{2}+\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}=1
and the above observations,
there is an
\eta>0
such that the right hand side of (
173
) decays as
e^{2\eta\varrho}
. In other words, there is a
constant
C
such that

(175)

|\theta^{-2}\bar{S}|\leq Ce^{2\eta\varrho}.

Next, define
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
by
[X_{B},X_{C}]=\lambda_{BC}^{A}X_{A}
. Due to (
156
), it is clear that
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
and

X_{D}(\lambda^{A}_{BC})
converge in
C^{0}
. Due to (
156
) and (
162
), it is clear that
X_{A}(\bar{\mu}_{B})

and
X_{B}X_{B}(\bar{\mu}_{C})
do not grow faster than polynomially. On the other hand,
e^{-\mu_{A}}
decays to zero exponentially due to
(
174
). Combining these observations with the formula
[
69
, (279), p. 69]
(with
\gamma^{A}_{BC}
replaced by
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
)
yields

(176)

\left|\theta^{-2}\bar{S}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{4}\sum}_{A,B,C}e^{2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{C}}(\lambda^{B}_{AC})^{2}\right|\leq C\langle\varrho\rangle^{a}e^{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{Sp}}\varrho}

on
M
. Combining this estimate with (
175
) yields the conclusion that there is an
\eta>0
and a
C
such that

(177)

\textstyle{\sum}_{A,B,C}e^{2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}}(\lambda^{A}_{BC})^{2}\leq Ce^{2\eta\varrho}

on
M
. Assume now that
\bar{x}\in\bar{M}
is such that
1+p_{A}(\bar{x})-p_{B}(\bar{x})-p_{C}(\bar{x})\leq 0
. Then, due to the continuity of the
p_{A}
, there is
an open neighbourhood
V
of
\bar{x}
such that
1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C}\leq\eta/2
in
V
. On the other hand, due to (
164
),

2\mu_{A}-2\mu_{B}-2\mu_{C}=2(1+p_{A}-p_{B}-p_{C})\varrho+O(1)\geq\eta\varrho-C

on
V
, for some constant
C
. Combining this estimate with (
177
) yields

(\lambda^{A}_{BC})^{2}\leq Ce^{\eta\varrho}

on
V
. This means that
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
converges to zero on
V
. On the other hand,
\lambda^{A}_{BC}
converges to
\gamma^{A}_{BC}
due to
(
156
). This proves the first statement of the theorem; note that
A
,
B
and
C
have to be distinct in order for the
statement not to be void, in which case
\mathscr{Y}^{A}([\mathscr{X}_{B},\mathscr{X}_{C}])=0
is equivalent to
\bar{\mathscr{Y}}^{A}([\bar{\mathscr{X}}_{B},\bar{\mathscr{X}}_{C}])=0
.

Next, note that
\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}=1
is an immediate consequence of the fact that
